- ARCADE IDENTITY IS A CONSTRAINT ON COMPUTED LISTS, NOT JUST ON COLOURS (2026-08-03). Two of the rail's material fixes needed a different SHAPE under Tabletop than under Arcade: grain wants two background layers per side where plain timber wants one, and a dashed stitch wants an `outline` where the old solid one was a `box-shadow`. Both would have shown up as Arcade diffs on an element that is 0px wide and paints nothing, which is the kind of diff it is tempting to wave through and the exact habit the sweep exists to prevent. Two cheap moves avoided it entirely and are worth reusing. The background longhands are held as token LISTS (`--gn-rail-face`, `-pos`, `-size`, `-repeat`) so the layer COUNT is a theme's business and Arcade keeps computing its four `none` layers. And Arcade's stitch colour is `currentcolor` rather than `transparent`, because `currentcolor` is the initial value of `outline-color`, so an outline at zero width and style `none` computes precisely what no outline at all computed. Result: 14284 values, nothing moved.
- ONE GREYSCALE TILE IS ALLOWED, AND THIS REVERSES STAGE 3 (2026-08-03, James's call, and the reasoning is his: "felt is a grey texture plus a colour, so one tile serves every pack"). Stage 3 ruled an image asset out and gave two reasons, and it is worth being precise about which one this overturns. It was NOT wrong that a tiling image is another request on a deliberately trimmed bundle — that cost is real and is now measured (32.9KB, fetched only under Tabletop, never by an Arcade session, asserted over CDP and bounded by a test). What it got wrong was the implicit assumption that an image asset is a per-pack cost, which is what made it look expensive: ten packs times one texture each is a bundle problem, and one tile times ten packs is a single 33KB file. THE THING THAT MAKES ONE TILE SUFFICIENT IS THAT IT IS GREY. It carries the weave and nothing else; the colour arrives at the use site as `--gn-texture-tint`, and `background-blend-mode: overlay` folds the two together — mid-grey being overlay's identity value is the whole mechanism, not a coincidence, and it is why the same cloth can be walnut in the shell and blue-green on a ping pong table. The corollary for stage 4: a pack that wants cloth sets a tint and a size, and MUST NOT ship a texture of its own. A second tile is the moment this decision stops being true.
- A PATTERN IS NOT A MATERIAL, AND AN EDGE IS WHAT SELLS A TABLE (2026-08-03, James: "it still does not feel like felt, and the cross-hatch is not cutting it"). Stage 3's felt was two crossed 45-degree gradients, which is a correct description of a weave and reads as a pattern anyway, because a weave at that scale is regular and cloth is not. The instinct is to keep tuning the weave. The thing that actually worked was the RAIL: a poker table reads as a table because the felt stops somewhere, and until it stopped, the app was a dark page with a texture on it rather than a surface with an edge. Worth logging as a general note for any future material work — before tuning the surface again, check whether the object has a boundary.
- THE TEXTURE GUARD MEASURES THE ENCODED BLOB, NOT THE CANVAS (2026-08-03): the generator's whole justification is that a contrast cost is measured rather than eyeballed, and for four iterations it measured the wrong thing. It sampled the canvas it had just drawn, then encoded to lossy WebP and shipped that. WebP was ringing the greys from 136 out to 147, so a tile that measured a 1.2% worst-case cost was shipping a 6.6% one — the guard was green about a file that did not exist yet. It now decodes its own output and measures that. THE GENERAL FORM, and this is the third time this repo has hit it: a check that runs on the input to a lossy step is not a check on the artefact. Two other metric errors from the same session are worth keeping for whoever regenerates the tile: an ABSOLUTE contrast bound is the wrong metric (it lets a high-contrast pair lose a lot and blocks a low-contrast pair from losing anything), so it is relative cost plus a floor per pair; and normalising the noise by its extreme outlier produced a standard deviation of 0.84 on a tile that looked flat, so it normalises to 2.5 sigma and clips.
- THE RAIL IS FIXED FURNITURE AND LIVES IN index.html, NOT IN REACT (2026-08-03). Two decisions in one, both load-bearing. (1) FIXED, not in flow: three TV layouts in this app are measured to the pixel against 1080 and one of them is already over, so a rail in flow would silently eat 28px of vertical budget and push a back button off a television. As a `position: fixed` overlay it costs zero layout, which was measured before and after across nine TV cases in both themes and came back byte-identical. (2) NOT A COMPONENT: it has no state, so rendering it from React buys nothing and costs two things — it would appear a frame late, after hydration, which on a phone reads as the furniture arriving after the felt; and as a second child of `#root` it would break the invariant that the safe-area rule depends on, which is that `#root` has exactly ONE child and that child is the route's shell (see the 2026-07-29 entry on `:where(#root) > *`). Sitting in `<body>` beside `#root` it exists before the first paint and leaves that invariant alone. The related implementation note: `border-image` was rejected before it was tried, because a border is layout and this must not be.
- A THEME'S FURNITURE IS INERT IN EVERY THEME THAT DOES NOT WANT IT (2026-08-03). The rail's seven tokens all exist on `:root` so the shell rules can read them unconditionally, and all seven are inert there: zero width, no timber, transparent colours. That inertness is the only reason one `<div class="gn-rail">` can sit in `index.html` for every theme without Arcade growing furniture, and it is exactly one careless "sensible default" away from being lost — so `theme-contrast.test.ts` asserts each one's Arcade value by name. Same shape as `--gn-glow-title: none` under Tabletop: a theme turns a structure token OFF rather than the markup being conditional, because markup that is conditional on the theme is markup the next theme has to remember.
- A TV LAYOUT IS ONLY FIXED WHEN THE LAST ELEMENT FITS, NOT WHEN THE CONTENT DOES (2026-08-02). The money board's overflow was measured twice, in two different sessions, against the FOOTER, and both times the answer was 116px more optimistic than the truth, because the back button sits below the footer and the fit was never asserted against it. That is not a slip, it is the natural thing to measure: the footer is the last thing that looks like content. So the rule from here: on any TV, the assertion is the bottom of the LAST RENDERED ELEMENT against the screen height, and on these screens that is the back button, which standing rule 4 puts there and which is useless off the bottom of a television. Casino Run's TV was measured the same way in its own session and has the same 116px error sitting in it today (see BUGS).
- THE THING BEING SIZED IS THE SCREEN, NOT THE COMPONENT (2026-08-02). The money board's density ladder was scoped as a function of the seat count, which is the obvious reading: the board is what grows. Measurement said otherwise. The craps hero costs 271.6px, about 1.7 board lines, and the balance warning another 118.3px, so a craps table with the tracker on and a table that did not balance ran off the bottom at TWO players, with a board that had barely started. A ladder keyed on seats alone can only handle that by tuning every rung for the worst case, which makes the common four-player blackjack night pay for a shooter panel it does not have. So the input is the whole screen's load in board-line equivalents and the overrides reach the hero, the warning, the header and the footer, not just the lines. The general form, worth remembering the next time something on a TV grows: FIT IS A PROPERTY OF THE SCREEN. A component that scales itself in isolation is measuring the wrong thing, and it will be measuring it correctly.
- A FIT CHECK MUST BE MADE TO FAIL BEFORE IT IS BELIEVED (2026-08-02, second time this has been written down). The modifier wall shipped broken with two green checks against it, so this session's harness re-injects the shipped metrics at runtime and asserts the fit checks FAIL at 6, 8 and 12 players before trusting them on the fixed layout, and the four new tests were each confirmed to fail with the thing they check removed: the `data-band` attribute stripped, a band's CSS deleted, the load argument dropped, and the ladder made to jump back to roomy at one seat count. The harness also reproduces the 2026-07-30 footer numbers to the pixel, which is what proves it is looking at the same screen rather than a different one that happens to fit.
- A deck rated by FEEL is wrong about half the time (2026-08-02): the playtest re-rated 21 of 36 cards, and the errors were not small — a severity 1 that ended runs outright, severity 3s that did nothing. Severity drives the draw weighting AND Casino Run's escalation, so a mis-rated deck makes the "gentle at first, nasty later" curve a lie. Ratings are now measured and bucketed by a test. The model is approximate and says so; what it buys is the ORDERING, which is what severity is for.
- CO-OP AND TEAMMATE RESULTS ARE A DISTINCT RIVALRY OUTCOME, NOT A TIE, and `side` is the primitive (2026-08-02, James's call). Casino Run writes an identical participant row for everyone on a run, so the placement comparison in `buildRivalry` scored a shared run as a DRAW between every pair of players in it. THE CHEAP FIX WAS REJECTED ON THE SPOT: skipping `format = "casino_run"` the way `isSeriesSummary` is skipped works today and is wrong the moment doubles ping pong, beer pong, cornhole or foosball land, because the same two people are teammates in one match and opponents in the next. Team structure cannot be a property of the PACK; it has to be a property of the MATCH. So `match_participants` gained a nullable `side text`. NULL MEANS NO TEAM STRUCTURE: every row written before the column existed and every free-for-all result forever, and null rows classify EXACTLY as they did before, which a brute-force test over every placement/isWinner pair asserts. A non-null string names the side; two players sharing a non-null value were teammates, different non-null values are opponents. The value is opaque and only ever compared for equality. `buildRivalry` gained a fourth outcome, `together`, checked AHEAD of win/loss/tie (it has to be, because the identical rows would otherwise reach the comparison and tie), counted separately in the totals and in the per-game breakdown, and SKIPPED by the meeting streak: counting it as a win would read a co-op night as an unbeaten run, and breaking the streak would claim something happened between two people who were not playing each other. BUILT AT CASINO RUN TIME ON PURPOSE, with Casino Run as the only writer this session, so the doubles formats already in IDEAS have the primitive waiting rather than having to invent it under deadline. Deploy: `ALTER TABLE match_participants ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS side text;`
- In a game where the bank must GROW, raising the minimum bet is a boon (2026-08-02): three cards were labelled banes for forcing bigger bets, which is help, not harm — bold play is optimal against a target. Two were given a real cost (the bank posts the ante dead once a leg); the third was relabelled. A wrong SIGN on a card is worse than a weak card: it is a lie on the screen the table is reading. The direction test now checks both betting styles, because checking bold play alone is exactly what missed it.
- Flat per-leg costs are lethal here, so money cards take a share (2026-08-02): a run must grow the bank ~75% over ~10-20 legs, so a card costing one ante a leg removes ~40% of the bank and ends the run on its own. That is why the rake and vig cards are shares of the pot or the win rather than multiples of the ante — and why a share of the win cannot be rendered as money, since the app never sees a hand.
- "Stake" is a banned word for anything except real-vs-play money (2026-08-02): `stakes` has meant that across the casino group since the flag shipped, and round three quietly introduced "the table stake" as the unit for bonus percentages. One word, two jobs, exactly as "floor" was doing before stages were named. The unit is now named per pack — the ANTE in a run, the BUY-IN at a cash table — and the card text prints it, so "10%" is never left as ten percent of something unstated.
- `appliesTo` has three cases, and the middle one earns its keep (2026-08-02): "any" (every table, co-op included), "cash" (needs per-player money, so never a co-op run), and a table list (that game, AND a run, because a run plays that game). Collapsing "cash" into the table list is what briefly put Mercy chip's free REBUY in front of a pack that has no buy-ins. "A run plays blackjack" and "a run has per-player money" are different claims and the type now says so.
- A run holds every table's cards, dormant until it plays that table (2026-08-02, James: "in casino run you might jump from roulette back to blackjack then to roulette"): the pack-specific half of the deck was unreachable in the only mode that plays all three games. Making the run's pool the union was the easy half; the useful half is saying WHEN each card bites, which is at the moment a game is picked for a leg, not on a tag nobody connects to anything.
- House rules are about the BOARD, not the room (2026-08-02, James: cards should be "more about the totals on the board and the money, not interaction like where the phone is or talking"). The first deck had six cards policing behaviour — no talking, phones off the table, last to sit antes. They are party rules wearing a casino costume: they never touch a number the app tracks, they are unenforceable without somebody appointing themselves referee, and one of them ("pays the table a forfeit") did not even name a cost. Retired and replaced with cards that move stakes and totals. RETIRED IDS ARE NEVER REUSED, asserted in a test: a run played under one still has rows in the ledger, and reusing the string would silently merge two different cards' histories.
- A card that mentions money must name a fraction, and the app renders it (2026-08-02): "pays a bonus" is an argument waiting to happen at a table with drinks on it. `bonusPct` plus `modifierRule()` turns it into a real figure against the table's own stake. This does NOT cross the line the group holds — the app is doing arithmetic on a number the table declared, exactly as it does for the minimum ante, not deciding whether a hand won. The unit is the live ante in Casino Run on purpose, so a card's cost climbs with the table.
- Severity pips needed a legend or they were decoration (2026-08-02): three dots with no key anywhere is a picture of a number nobody can read. They are now filled/empty out of three with a printed key on every screen that shows them, plus a tooltip and screen-reader text. Worth logging because it is the second time a "self-evident" visual in this pack turned out not to be.
- A co-op run has to be LOSABLE, and the first cut was not (2026-08-01, James: "there appears to be no way to lose, it just keeps giving more attempts which seems like no fun"). He was right, and the simulation put a number on it: a table betting the minimum took ~1,100 legs to resolve and half a percent of runs never resolved at all. The original rule — a missed stage costs the attempt and nothing else — made the leg budget decorative and left the floor as the only fail state, which a hovering bank dodges forever. `attemptsPerStage` is the fix, and the reason it is the RIGHT fix rather than just a harder one is that it is aimed: it takes a grinder from 1,100 legs to 15 while moving bold play by under a point, so it ends the behaviour that was un-fun without changing what a committed table experiences. The ante rising on every miss is the same pressure applied gently — grinding gets more expensive before it gets fatal.
- Tracking a minimum ante is bookkeeping, not refereeing (2026-08-01): worth stating because it sits close to the line the whole casino group holds. The app never computes whether a bet WON — that is the humans' job and it does not change. What it can do is keep a number the table already agreed on and apply the rises the cards call for, which is the same kind of work as counting legs or stages. If a future session wants the app to compute what a modifier PAYS, that is the line, and it is still where it was.
- One-shot tokens live in the leg log (2026-08-01): a purchase is an entry that moved the bank, so undo pops it and everything — the refund, the un-holding of the card, the ante relief it cancelled — falls out of the same re-derivation that already existed. A separate `heldTokens` array would have been a second thing for undo to unwind and the first place the two could disagree. Buys are marked `kind: "buy"` and absent means "leg", so no migration was needed. Deliberately NOT blocked on affordability: spending the last of the bank on a hedge is a legitimate and in-genre way to end a run, and the floor check catches it like anything else.
- Casino Run is play money, full stop (2026-08-01, James's call): the quotas climb to multiples of the starting bank and the hardest ladder is designed to be unwinnable, so a real-money option would be offering people a losing proposition by design. The selector is gone and the server ignores a `stakes` body rather than honouring one, so a stale client cannot open a real-money run. The `stakes` field stays on the state because every money screen in the group is built around the stakes-aware formatter, and threading a special case through all of them to save one constant would be worse than the constant.
- The modifier deck is 50/50 by assertion, not by intention (2026-08-01): it shipped 11 boons to 13 banes, and the "any" pool — the ONLY pool Casino Run draws from — was 4 to 8. So the pack whose whole texture comes from random draws was being punished by them twice as often as it was helped, and nothing in the app would ever have said so. The split is now enforced overall and within the any pool, and every pack is asserted to own at least one card of each kind.
- Modifiers are DECLARED, never enforced (2026-07-30, James's brief, restated here because it is the line a future session will try to cross): the app shows a modifier's rule and records that it was live; it never applies its effect. The temptation is obvious — "blackjack pays double" is a payout rule and the app already knows the bets when the tracker is on — and giving in costs the whole group. Enforcing a payout means knowing what every hand paid, which means logging every hand, which means the tracker stops being optional and the minimal-input promise the four casino packs are built on is gone. The trade as it stands: one tap at setup buys the rule on the TV all night and win rate per card for free. If a future session genuinely wants enforced modifiers, the honest move is a new, explicitly high-input format that says so, not a flag on this one.
- A modifier `id` is permanent, everything else about a card is not (2026-07-30): ids go into `match_participants.meta`, so renaming one does not error — it orphans that card's entire history and the stat simply vanishes from the panel. Names, rule text, kind, severity and `appliesTo` are display or selection concerns and are free to change any time; only the id is frozen, and the 24 shipped ids are pinned in a test whose comment says why. Same rule and same reason as `ledger`, `gameName` and `keyPrefix` in packs.ts. The read path renders an unknown id AS THE ID rather than as a blank, so a retired card's history stays visible instead of quietly becoming empty rows.
- The ids go on the PARTICIPANT, not on the match (2026-07-30): the brief said "do not stuff a list into `matches.label`" and it is right twice over. `label` is one display string that Mario Party already uses for the board, so a list there would need parsing and would collide. More importantly the grain is wrong: the stat is win rate and net PER PLAYER per modifier, so the ids have to sit beside that player's own net or every read becomes a join. Writing them to every participant is redundant across a session's rows and that redundancy is the point — it needs no schema change and answers the question directly. A night with no cards omits the key entirely rather than writing `[]`, so a plain night's meta bag stays byte-for-byte what it was before modifiers existed and the read layer has one encoding of "none", not two.
- Per-modifier money is PER PLAYER, and the panel admits it is correlation (2026-07-30): a crew-wide net per modifier reads as zero on every player-banked night, because the table is zero-sum by construction — that is what the balance check enforces. So the only honest grain for money is one person's own nights. And since the app never applies a modifier, the panel cannot claim a card caused anything; it reports how the nights carrying it went, and says so in its own copy. Three nights at 100% is three nights, and a bare percentage would invite a crew to retire a card over noise.
- The draw takes a deck, a count, a filter and a weighting (2026-07-30): `drawModifiers` could have been `drawTwoForSetup()`, which is all this session needed. It is not, because Casino Run — the very next queued item — draws on clearing a quota (escalating, which is a weight override), draws a forced bane on missing one (a kind filter) and deals a hand of three for draft mode. Those are the same function with different arguments, and hard-coding the setup case would have meant writing it again three times. The awkward cases are pinned now rather than when that session hits them: asking for more than exists returns the whole pool instead of looping, a filter that empties the pool returns nothing instead of throwing, and a weighting that zeroes the pool falls back to uniform instead of spinning.
- Co-op is CASINO RUN, its own pack, not a format inside the existing ones (2026-07-30, James's design): the earlier backlog entry described co-op as a mode you could switch a blackjack table into. That does not work. `settleCash` is built on per-player net summing to zero against a banker, and a co-op run replaces that with one shared pot measured against a target — the central assumption, inverted. Serving both from one engine means a branch through every function that four packs depend on, in service of the one caller that disagrees with it. As its own pack it reuses what it genuinely shares (integer cents, the money inputs, the `--cg-*` tokens, the modifier deck) and owns its own settlement. THE COST IS REAL AND ACCEPTED: a co-op leg played at blackjack lands under Casino Run, not Blackjack, so it does not appear in blackjack's lifetime money. That is the right trade — the alternative is co-op rows polluting a per-player net stat they do not obey — but it is a loss, and the pack's own copy should say so rather than leaving people to discover it.
- Wins unify across stakes, only money splits (2026-07-30, James's call, and it REVERSES an earlier answer): the first instinct was to keep play money entirely separate — its own totals, its own record, its own everything — and that is wrong in a way that only shows up in use. A win is a win. You either finished the night up or you did not, and whether the chips were worth anything does not change what happened at the table, so splitting the win rate would quietly halve somebody's record the day the crew played a pretend night. What mixing genuinely corrupts is the TOTALS: adding a $60 real net to an 80-play-chip one produces a confident number that is arithmetic nonsense. So the line is drawn exactly there. Nights, win rate, streaks, rebuys, hours and nights banked count once; net, staked, averages, ROI, best, worst and net-per-hour are computed twice from the same rows. The implementation detail that enforces it: the aggregate has NO combined net field at all, so a later screen cannot render one by accident — the only way to get a total is to ask for a stakes.
- One games row, no second leaderboard tab (2026-07-30): the tempting implementation of play money is a second `games` row per pack ("Blackjack (play)"), because then every existing reader splits for free. It was rejected, and the reason is that it would split the wrong thing. `games.name` is the JOIN KEY for the crew leaderboard tabs, so a second row means two tabs, two histories, two of every unified stat, and a player's Blackjack record silently becoming two records — which is precisely the outcome the registry's own warning about `gameName` exists to prevent, and precisely the opposite of "wins unify". Stakes therefore rides in `match_participants.meta` next to the money already there, `ensureGame` is untouched, `stats.ts` is untouched, and the split happens in the one place that should know about it: the pack's own stats panel. The cost is that the split is a read-layer concern rather than a free consequence of the schema, which is the right cost to pay.
- An absent stakes value means REAL, and that decision is load-bearing (2026-07-30): every ledger row written before this shipped carries no stakes, and every `formatCents` call site outside the casino passes no stakes. Both had to keep meaning dollars, so the parameter is optional everywhere and absent is real rather than "unknown". Defaulting the other way would have moved a crew's entire real-money history into play money overnight, silently, with no migration to notice. The server picks the same direction for the same reason: anything that is not literally the word "play" in a start body is real, because a malformed request recording a real night as pretend is the more damaging of the two mistakes. Both directions are named tests.
- Two signals for play money, and up/down keep their colours (2026-07-30): the brief asked for a distinct prefix plus a different colour token so the two can never be confused across a room. The prefix is `P$` — plain ASCII, so it survives a TV browser, a screenshot and the canvas recap card, where a currency glyph might not — and the colour is a violet badge on the pack page and the TV header. What was NOT done is tinting the money itself: green-up and red-down mean DIRECTION, not currency, and hijacking them for stakes would have traded one confusion for a worse one. The badge is the signal you catch without reading a number, which is the case the requirement was actually about; the prefix is the one that survives being photographed. The related implementation call is `money(stakes)`, a bound formatter each casino screen takes ONCE: threading a stakes argument through twenty individual call sites is exactly the kind of list this repo has been bitten by, and a missed one renders play money as dollars. The browser suite now asserts the invariant directly — on a play table, a `$` not preceded by a `P` is a bug — because no unit test can see a component that forgot to ask.
- Craps' hands are DERIVED from an event log, which is the only reason its undo is correct (2026-07-30): the session brief called undo "the easiest thing in the session to get subtly wrong", and it was right about why. Undoing a seven-out has to do two things at once — reopen the closed hand with everything it had, and hand the dice back to whoever sevened out — and the natural implementation keeps a roll counter plus a `shooterId` and unwinds both by hand. That is right until the day it isn't: undo twice across a shooter change, or undo a point instead of a roll, and the counter and the dice disagree with each other in a way nothing errors about. So the log is the truth and `crapsHands()` is the only thing that reads it, exactly as Smashdown's burn board is only ever written by `burnedFrom(games)`. Undo pops one event and the hand reopens on its own; the shooter comes straight off the popped event rather than being recomputed. The one piece of state carried alongside is `shooterId`, because after a seven-out the dice belong to somebody with no events yet — and even that is restored from the event rather than guessed. Two smaller calls fell out of the same model. A SEVEN-OUT IS NOT A ROLL, so "rolls survived before sevening out" means what it says and an instant seven-out is a hand of zero rather than one. And handing the dice on voluntarily is logged as a `pass` that CLOSES the hand, because twelve rolls are twelve rolls whether or not they ended badly, and a hand that never closed would otherwise vanish from the record.
- The live longest-hand number excludes the hand in progress; the ledger includes it (2026-07-30): the TV shows the current shooter's roll count next to "to beat", and if the open hand counted towards the record those two numbers would be the same the moment somebody took the lead — a target that is always exactly what you have already done is not a target. So live reads take closed hands only. At completion the night is over, which means the hand that was in progress is a hand that happened, so `crDetail(..., { includeOpen: true })` is what the ledger writes. Same shape as `settleCash({ final: true })` treating an absent cash-out as a bust only once the night ends, and for the same reason: "unknown" and "finished" are different states and the boundary between them is the moment the host taps End.
- Craps CAN type its longest roll and roulette cannot type its streak, and the difference is real (2026-07-30): the group rule says a stat is either reconstructable — so it gets a box on the cash-out form — or it is not, and then it is absent rather than invented. Roulette's winning streak got no box, deliberately, because nobody honestly remembers a run of spins. Craps' longest roll gets one, because a table ARGUES about how long somebody held the dice and remembers the answer; it is the one number in the pack everybody at it already knows. Applying the roulette precedent mechanically would have made the tracker mandatory for the pack's headline stat, which is the opposite of the minimal-input rule. The tell for future packs: ask whether the room would still know the number tomorrow, not whether the app could have computed it.
- Longest roll is a CREW record, not a personal stat (2026-07-30, James's call): every other detail stat in this group is per-player and sits on a player's own subline. Longest roll gets its own headline on the crew leaderboard naming who holds it, because that is how it works at a real table — the number people know is who has held the dice longest, full stop, and burying it inside one player's row would lose the thing that makes it worth tracking. Mechanically it is free: `cashLifetimeStats` already returns every row's meta bag, so the panel takes a max across all players with no new query and no new endpoint. `CasinoPanel` grew one optional `record` slot for it, which poker's biggest pot will probably want too.
- Picker grouping lives on the CLIENT list, not the pack registry (2026-07-30): the obvious place for a `group` field is `SESSION_PACKS`, and it is the wrong one. That registry describes packs that run a server-side session; the picker shows two things that are not — Tournament, which has no session, no route segment and no ws type, and Beerio Kart, which is a FORMAT under the Mario Kart tile rather than a tile at all. Grouping has to be where every tile lives together, which is `PACKS` in apps/web/src/packs.ts. The related decision is the comment: `group` is marked DISPLAY ONLY in the type, because this repo has three fields that are permanent and silent when changed (ledger, gameName, keyPrefix) and somebody reading a fourth string field in a pack definition should not have to work out which kind it is. An empty group renders nothing, so the next pack joins a section by setting one field.
- Mario Party gave up the die (2026-07-30): 🎲 was Mario Party's emoji and craps has the stronger claim on it — a die IS craps, whereas Mario Party's own scoring unit is the star it now carries. Worth a line only because "never change a pack's identifiers" is a rule in this file, and this looks like breaking it: it is not. Emoji is DERIVED from the registry at render time and never written to the database, so changing it repaints every screen at once and orphans nothing. The three that are permanent are ledger, gameName and keyPrefix, and the registry says so where somebody will read it. Roulette's entry also lost the comment explaining that it settled for 🎡 because the die was taken, since that is no longer why.
- The casino screens and money routes were extracted on the SECOND example, not the first (2026-07-29, the roulette session): blackjack shipped with its money board, setup screen, live table and every money route inline, and a note saying roulette would decide whether to extract them. That was the right order and it is worth defending, because the tempting version is to build the abstraction first. Extracting on one example is guessing at what generalises; building roulette made the answer trivial to see, and it was not the answer a guess would have given. The board turned out to be identical LINE FOR LINE, with only a brand, a header clause and a per-player subline differing — three props — while the thing that looked most likely to be shared, the tracker, turned out to be the one genuinely per-pack piece (a blackjack hand is a bet and a result, a roulette spin is a bet type and won/lost, a craps roll is neither). The evidence that it worked: `BlackjackPage` fell from 17.6 kB to 5.0 kB and blackjack's stylesheet from ~200 lines to ~20, while roulette landed at 5.4 kB with no copied structure at all. The regression proof was the browser suite blackjack already had: 35 checks written against its behaviour BEFORE the refactor, re-run after it, plus 28 more for roulette and the buy-ins. Two of the 35 failed and both were the checks pinning copy the shared screen deliberately reworded, which is exactly the failure a good regression suite should produce — it noticed, and the noticing was cheap to read.
- Sharing the casino ROUTES does not contradict "routes stay per-pack" (2026-07-29): pack-runtime.ts states plainly that each pack keeps its own routes and request validation, because "a shared validator would drift into a lowest common denominator". `casino-runtime.ts` shares routes anyway, and the distinction is real rather than a convenience. That earlier call was about Smash vs Mario Kart vs Ping Pong, whose bodies genuinely differ — a fighter pick, a race result, a game winner — and where a shared shape would have to be invented. The casino packs are a SUB-FAMILY with one data model, already shared and already tested: the body of a rebuy is `{ playerId }` in all four, not by coincidence but because they are all the same cash game with different games attached. Sharing there is sharing one idea, not flattening four. The line was still drawn: the tracker routes stay in each pack file, and they are the routes whose bodies actually differ. If a fifth casino-shaped pack ever wants a different money model, the honest move is to stop calling it a casino pack rather than to add a flag to this runtime.
- A per-player buy-in FOLLOWS the table default until it is touched (2026-07-29, James's request): the obvious implementation copies the default into each seat when the seat is added, and it is wrong in a way that only shows up in use. A host sets $20, adds four people, then decides the night is a $40 night — and with copied values, nothing moves, so they have to edit four boxes and will miss one. Tracking the override as `null` = "follow the default" makes the common case free and the deliberate case explicit, and it needs no extra UI: the seat says "on the default" or "their own $100" with a way back. The React detail worth keeping is that the input's `key` carries the current default while a seat is untouched, so changing the default remounts and re-seeds it, and becomes stable the moment the seat has its own number — the alternative, a controlled input reset by an effect, fights the user's half-typed text. The server side of this had ALREADY SHIPPED with blackjack (`buyIns`, keyed by roster index) and was reachable by nothing, which is its own small lesson: a validated endpoint with no screen is not a feature, and it sat there for a day looking like one.
- Roulette's two stats are different IN KIND, and that is the group rule made concrete (2026-07-29): favourite bet type is reconstructable — anyone can say "I was on red all night" — so it is a typed field on the cash-out form AND derived from the tracker, with typed winning. Max consecutive winning spins is NOT reconstructable, because a streak is an ordering fact and nobody remembers it honestly at 1am, so it requires the tracker, is null rather than zero when the tracker was off, and has DELIBERATELY NO BOX to type it into. That last part is the actual decision. Offering a field would have been friendlier and would have quietly filled the ledger with guesses, and a lifetime "best run" stat assembled from guesses is worse than one that admits it only knows the nights somebody was counting. A test asserts that a `bestStreak` smuggled into a typed detail bag is ignored on read, so a later well-meaning change cannot open the door by accident. Zero would have been the other tempting answer and is the worse lie: it claims the player never won twice in a row.
- The safe-area inset is structural now, because the hand-written list did exactly what hand-written lists here always do (2026-07-29, found by James on a phone within hours of the blackjack deploy): the iOS shell paints under the status bar by design (`viewport-fit=cover` plus a black-translucent status bar), and every root shell is supposed to inset its own CONTENT with `env(safe-area-inset-*)` while its background still bleeds edge to edge. That rule was applied by listing the shell class names, and blackjack shipped without `.bj-root` on the list, so the entire pack sat under the clock and its back button was unreachable. It is the same silent-registration shape as the `ping_pong` ledger typo and the `beerio` emoji key: self-consistent code, nothing to compile against, no error, and only findable by holding a real device. The fix could have been one more class name; it is `:where(#root) > *` instead, which is a precise description of "the shell" because every route renders exactly one element as #root's child, and which a new pack cannot fail to be covered by. The `:where()` is load-bearing and worth stating: an `#id` in the selector would outrank every class, so a plain `#root > *` would have SILENTLY REPLACED the padding on the five TV screens that set their own `calc()` insets — trading a bug on one pack for a subtler one on five. Zero specificity keeps this a floor that anything can override, which is what a default should be. The remaining hand-maintained half is deliberate: a screen that sets its own padding must fold the insets into its own calc(), and that IS forgettable — `.pp-tv` had never done it and was found by this sweep. It is a smaller surface (only TV screens do it) and it degrades to "a TV has slightly wrong padding" rather than "a phone's back button is unreachable", which is the right place to accept the risk.
- THE UNIT IS CURRENCY, and this SUPERSEDES the bragging-rights-only entries (2026-07-28, James's call; implemented 07-29 with blackjack). Two places in this file said the opposite and are now updated rather than left to contradict each other: the "Wager ledger" idea, which read "bragging rights only, never money", and the DEFERRED line "Money wagers: regulatory mess. Bragging rights only." The second is kept STRUCK THROUGH rather than deleted, because the reasoning it rested on is still the boundary and deleting it would lose that. THE DISTINCTION THAT MAKES THIS FINE, and it is the whole argument: the app RECORDS WHAT A HOME GAME ALREADY DID. It does not facilitate a wager, hold a balance, transfer anything, settle anyone up, or take a cut. A cash night's ledger row is a note of what happened, in the same way the recap card is a note of who won — the money moved between people at a table, in cash, with or without this app. Everything that would actually MOVE money (payments, escrow, settle-up transfers, a payout button) stays deferred on exactly the original grounds, and that line is worth holding: it is the difference between a scorekeeper and a payment processor, and it is much easier to hold now than after someone has asked for a Venmo button. Recording both halves because a superseded decision with no successor entry reads as an oversight three sessions later, and a superseding one with no statement of what still holds reads as a licence.
- RIVALRIES INCLUDE CASINO NIGHTS, casino-banked ones too, WITH the caveat stated (2026-07-28, James's call; implemented 07-29). Because placement is derived from net rank, a cash night produces ordinary `match_participants` rows and every existing reader picks them up for free — including the rivalry pages, which will now show two people as having "met" on a night they were both playing against the house rather than against each other. On a player-banked table that is defensible: the banker genuinely is the other side of every hand, and finishing above someone on the night is a real comparison. ON A CASINO-BANKED TABLE IT IS THE WEAKER CLAIM, and that is the caveat: the players were not strictly playing against each other at all, they were each playing the building, and their relative net is closer to a shared circumstance than to a head-to-head. James's call was to include it anyway rather than special-case it. Logged here with the caveat spelled out for one specific reason: if the rivalry pages later read oddly — someone's record against a friend swinging on nights they never competed — the reason is ON RECORD, and the fix is a one-line filter (skip rows whose `meta.bank === "casino"` in the rivalry MEETING map, which is already the one place head-to-head encounters are counted) rather than an investigation into where phantom meetings came from.
- Money is integer cents, and the balance check is why that is not a style preference (2026-07-29): every amount in the casino group — session jsonb, `match_participants.meta`, request bodies, the client's own state — is an integer number of cents, and dollars exist only at two edges. The reason is specific rather than general hygiene. The zero-sum check asks whether a table's nets sum to EXACTLY zero, and in binary floating point they routinely do not: 3.33 + 3.33 + 3.34 is 10.000000000000002, so a night that was entirely correct would have announced itself as off by two ten-billionths of a cent and sent the host hunting a mistake that did not exist. That is a miserable failure to debug months later, when the only evidence is a warning nobody can reproduce. So `parseCents` parses from the DIGITS rather than through `parseFloat` (exact by construction, not exact because `Math.round` usually rescues it), the server's `cents()` REJECTS a non-integer rather than rounding it (rounding would hide a client that had started doing dollar arithmetic, which is the exact regression this is guarding against), and there is a named canary test built from many small odd amounts summing to a round total that fails the moment anyone reintroduces a float. One small thing found while writing it: a table that comes out exactly even produces `-0` for the derived banker, which compares equal to zero everywhere but prints as "-0" on a TV, so it is normalised.
- The ledger unit is the SESSION, and that is what let the casino group land with no schema change (2026-07-29): the obvious alternative was a row per hand, which every card-tracking app does. It was never on the table here. One completed night is ONE `matches` row plus one `match_participants` row per player, and PLACEMENT IS DERIVED by ranking net descending — which means a cash night is indistinguishable from any other night to every reader in the app. The crew leaderboard, the rivalry pages, the recap card, the profile aggregates, `feedAgg`, guest linking and the night recap all worked on day one with zero changes to `stats.ts`, and the whole money model rides in `match_participants.meta`, a column that already existed for Mario Party's bonus stars. The temptation to resist, and it will come back with poker: adding a money COLUMN to the ledger. It would be one migration and it would quietly make every casino pack a special case in the highest-traffic file in the repo. Money in meta, placement from net, and the packs stay ordinary.
- The banker's net is derived, so the balance check is checking something else entirely (2026-07-29): stated plainly because the obvious reading of "the table must sum to zero" is wrong. On a player-banked table the banker IS the other side of every hand, so their net is arithmetically the inverse of everyone else's; deriving it means the table sums to zero BY CONSTRUCTION and there is nothing an arithmetic check could ever catch. What the check actually compares is the banker's own count of their rack — which a real banker does at the end of the night anyway — against what the players' numbers say it should be, and the delta is the sum of every typed net. That is a genuinely useful thing to know ("off by $17.25, so a cash-out is too high") and no other pack in this app can check its own correctness at all. Three consequences worth keeping. (1) The check is SKIPPED, not failed, when the banker has not cashed out: an absent count is not a count of zero, and treating it as one would report every table where the players collectively finished up as broken, which is most of them mid-night. (2) It is a WARNING, NOT A VETO — the host is told the exact figure and can still record the night, because the app records what a home game did rather than refereeing it. What it must never do is materialize numbers it already knows disagree WITHOUT saying so, hence 409-then-force rather than either silence or a hard block. (3) The banker's typed cash-out never reaches the ledger; the derived net does. Their own count is the cross-check, not the record.
- The tracker is opt-in and must never be the only way to learn something (2026-07-29): the live tracker defaults OFF for every casino pack and the host can flip it on mid-session. The rule that makes that safe is that the tracker being off cannot lose a stat the cash-out form could have captured, so blackjack's three details (biggest bet, biggest win, blackjacks hit) are BOTH derived from tracked hands AND typed boxes on the cash-out form, prefilled from the tracker when it ran. The subtlety that took a second pass: typed beats derived PER FIELD, not per player. Doing it per player means a host who corrects the biggest bet silently throws away the blackjack count the tracker had been keeping all night, which is a data loss nobody would notice. The related call: turning the tracker OFF keeps the hands already recorded, because they are what those details are derived from and deleting them would lose stats the host had already collected by accident. What is genuinely allowed to be tracker-only is the stuff that cannot be reconstructed after the fact at all — a roulette winning streak, a craps roll length — and those are simply absent on a night the tracker was off, rather than being faked from a total.
- A cash game is not a fixed roster (2026-07-29): every other pack in this app locks its roster at start, because a Smash night has the players it has. A cash table does not work that way — somebody turns up at eleven and sits down, and somebody else busts and leaves — so blackjack has an add-player route and a reopen ("sat back down") route, and a player who never cashed out is treated as still holding chips rather than as a result. That last one is the interesting split: LIVE, an absent cash-out means "unknown", and reporting `-totalIn` would paint a losing night for someone whose chips are on the table. Only at COMPLETION does it become zero, which is the honest reading (you busted and walked), and the End Session confirm names everyone it is about to record that way rather than doing it quietly. Without the split the live TV money board would have shown every player as deeply down until the moment they left, which is precisely backwards.
- One exclusion, at the one place every counter already goes through (2026-07-28, the series-row session): the series summary row is the first row in this ledger that describes rows already in it, and getting the exclusion wrong is invisible — nobody spots that their games-played is one too high per series. The choice was between filtering at each reader and filtering inside `feedAgg`. It went inside feedAgg, because the crew leaderboard was already unified onto that one aggregate precisely so two screens could not disagree about a player, and putting the rule at the shared chokepoint means the NEXT caller inherits it rather than being the one that forgets. What that does not cover is the tallies that never call feedAgg, so those were hunted rather than assumed, and the hunt found one that reading the plan would not have: the RIVALRY MEETING MAP, built in the same loop as feedAgg but after it, which would have counted a series as an extra head-to-head encounter on top of every battle inside it and quietly broken both players' records. The recap rollup was the other: a series row shares its battles' sessionKey ON PURPOSE (that is what makes the deferred-backfill argument work), so it would have landed in their unit and told the room "won 4 of 6 games" about a five-battle series. Both are named in the code next to their `continue`. The regression test that matters most is the negative one: a `bo3` row must still count, because a careless "skip labelled rows" fix would delete Best Of, Mario Party and Grand Prix from everyone's stats just as silently.
- The series row is written by reconciling, and an abandoned series writes nothing (2026-07-28): `over` can flip in three places, and only one of them is the obvious one. The battle that completes the count is obvious; an undo taking it back is easy to remember; the MERCY TOGGLE ending a series with nothing played at all is the one a "write the row when the last battle lands" rule misses, and it would have shipped as "mercy ends the night but nobody is recorded as winning it". So `syncSeriesRow` asks one question — does the ledger agree with `smashdownStatus` right now — and every route that can change the answer calls it. Both halves are idempotent, so it is safe to call twice or on the wrong format. The other call: a series the host ENDS EARLY writes no row. Best Of finalizes an abandoned set to the game leader, and that is right there, because the games inside a set are never materialized and abandoning would lose them entirely. Smashdown is the opposite: every battle is already a row, so an abandoned series loses nothing, and naming a "winner" of a series that did not finish would be inventing a result rather than rescuing one. NO BACKFILL ROUTE was built, which the plan called for, and the honest reason is that there is nothing to backfill: the deferral lasted one session on the same day, so the only sessions that can predate the series row are ones started in the hours between two deploys, and those write their row the moment the host taps End (complete reconciles too). Building a one-shot admin route for zero rows is machinery pretending to be caution. If real history is ever imported, the mechanism already exists: run `syncSeriesRow` over `sessionsForGroup`.
- Smashdown is a FORMAT, and the burn board is DERIVED (2026-07-28): the two calls that shaped the session. (1) It is a fourth entry in `SmashFormat`, not a fifth pack. It shares the roster, the title selector, the character system, the FFA ledger unit, the runtime and the TV route, and the only things it adds are three state fields and a set of rules. Building it as a pack would have meant a registry entry, a route segment, a server file and a ledger key for something that is one `format` string on the rows it already writes — and it would have split Smash's history across two leaderboard tabs, which is exactly what the pack registry's own warning about `gameName` says never to do. (2) The burn board is stored on the session (the TV and the pack page both render it, and it is the centrepiece) but it is only ever WRITTEN by `burnedFrom(games)`. That is what makes undo correct by construction rather than by care: the alternative, subtracting the undone battle's fighters from a maintained set, is right until two battles use the same fighter or a legacy row carries a null, and then the board silently overstates how much of the roster is gone. Recompute, never patch — the same reasoning the KOTH undo has always used for its throne. The related rule: after a battle the picks are cleared so the next one is a fresh choice, EXCEPT on the last battle, where they stay put so every screen can still show what the series finished on and an undo has something to put back.
- Mercy fires on "exceeds", never on "can no longer be caught" (2026-07-28): the natural way to write the clinch check is "no chaser can reach the leader", and in most formats that is the same sentence. It is not the same here, because a tie at the top is a CO-WIN rather than something to break, so a chaser who can still draw level can still change the result. Mercy therefore needs the leader's total to strictly EXCEED every other player's total plus every remaining battle, and the boundary case — leader on 2, chaser on 0, two battles left — must NOT end the series. That case is a named test in both directions, because the failure is invisible: the series just ends a battle early and hands one player a win that was still a shared one. Two smaller calls in the same shape. A clinch is reported even when the mercy toggle is OFF, so the screen can say the lead is unbeatable without acting on it, which is the honest split between a fact and a rule. And the toggle is settable MID-SERIES rather than only at setup: a crew that decides at battle four they have somewhere to be should not have to abandon the night to act on it, and defaulting it off means the surprising behaviour is always the one someone opted into.
- The battle cap is a title property, said out loud before the night starts (2026-07-28): `floor(fighters / players)` looks like a rounding detail and is actually the shape of the format. In Ultimate four players get 21 battles and nobody ever meets the cap; in Smash 64 the same four get THREE. So the cap is computed from the SELECTED title's roster, the number input is clamped to it, and the setup screen states it as a sentence naming the title, the fighter count and the limit, rather than letting the host type 10 and finding out at battle four. A cap of zero (more players than the title can feed, which Smash 64 reaches at nine) is treated as a real answer and blocks the start with the same sentence, not as an error to round up to one. The server clamps independently of the client, and clamps rather than rejects: a client that sends a stale number gets the series that is actually possible instead of a 400 the host cannot act on. The related roster call: Smashdown is capped at 8 players even though the pack's roster allows 16, because everyone plays every battle (that is what makes the cap arithmetic true) and a battle is an FFA game, which `validateFfa` caps at 8. Allowing more would have produced a series that could not be recorded.
- The series ledger row was split off, and the split is safe because of the sessionKey (2026-07-28; SHIPPED the same day in the next session, which is the outcome this entry predicted): James wanted a series-level row labeled `smashdown`, kept out of headline wins and shown as its own "series won" stat. It was not in the Smashdown session, and the reason was blast radius rather than difficulty: `ResultRow` in stats.ts does not carry `matches.label`, so adding it means the five queries that build it, the `Agg` shape, `feedAgg`, `finishAgg` and five client surfaces — the highest-traffic file in the repo, in the same session as a new format. What makes deferring free rather than lossy is that every battle in one series already shares a `sessionKey` and the ledger key is `{prefix}:{eventId}:{sessionKey}:{idx}`, so the follow-up session can group battles by `sessionKey` and derive every series that has already been played. Nothing in this session touches that grouping, which is the one constraint the deferral rests on and the reason it is stated here as well as in NEXT UP. Recording this because a half-built feature with no written reason is indistinguishable from an oversight three sessions later.
- One registry for pack identity, and the two bugs that proved it was overdue (2026-07-28): the argument for this was theoretical when it was raised — two spellings, nothing connecting them, and the next pack a coin flip. Building it turned up two live bugs, both already shipped, both silent, both exactly the predicted shape. The event TV resolver and the event detail payload each mapped "ping_pong", which is not a spelling that exists anywhere in this app, so Ping Pong sessions were dropped by a lookup that gates on a hit: the TV sat on the lobby while ping pong was being played, and the tile never said "live now". The recap card's emoji table was keyed "beerio" against a `games.pack` of "beerio_kart", so every Beerio night has drawn a trophy instead of a beer since the pack landed. Neither threw. Neither would ever have been found by reading the code that contained it, because each file was self-consistent; they were only findable by putting the two sides next to each other, which is the same lesson the Mario Party 409 gap taught in cleanup phase 6. Worth recording the aggravating detail honestly: the TV session that shipped the first bug ran INTO this problem on its first day (it needed to return a pack identifier the client switches on) and solved it by writing two more one-off maps instead of stopping to build the registry. The recommendation to do the map session first was right, and taking it in the other order cost a live bug. THE LINE HELD ON WHAT NOT TO DERIVE: the lazy route imports in App.tsx and the router mounts in index.ts stay hand-written, because Vite needs static literals inside import() to split a chunk at all, and because mount order is load-bearing (public TV routers before the authed ones, or requireAuth 401s the request before it can fall through). Both are named in the pack checklist above rather than pretended away. prefetch.ts is the instructive middle case: its importers must stay literal for Vite, but typing the table `Record<PackKey | "beerio", Importer>` moves the failure from "a tap feels slower and nobody knows why" to a compile error, which is the whole game here — the goal was never to delete registration, it was to make the registration that remains impossible to get silently wrong.
- The event TV auto-follows instead of being told what to show (2026-07-28): the 07-25 request specified an active-pack POINTER on the event (a nullable column set on start and cleared on end) with the TV subscribing to it, plus a single-active-pack rule to keep the pointer honest. That was dropped in favour of reading what is actually happening: every candidate already carries a status and an updatedAt, so "the most recently touched non-completed session" is derivable with no new state, nothing to set, nothing to forget to clear, and nothing that can disagree with reality. The pointer version has a specific failure this one cannot have: a host who ends a night by closing the tab leaves the pointer set, and the TV then insists on a game nobody is playing until someone clears it by hand. The cost of auto-follow is the opposite failure, following the wrong session when two are genuinely live at once, and that resolves itself the moment anyone touches the one they care about. If the surprise ever actually bites on a real night, an override is a small addition on top; building it first would have been guessing. The single-active-pack enforcement and the bounce-everyone-to-the-hub broadcast were dropped WITH the pointer and moved back to FEATURES on their own terms, because they were only ever infrastructure for the pointer, and whether phones should be yanked between screens mid-night is a separate question that deserves its own answer.
- The TV waiting screen has two jobs, and the second one is the recap (2026-07-28, James after testing on a real TV): it was built as the pre-game holding screen and it is also what is on between every game, which is the busier of the two states and the one where people actually look at the TV. It now splits on whether the night has any results yet, and the between-games face is the recap: standings with the leader crowned, and the latest results. The implementation call worth keeping is that it does NOT compute any of that: the recap route's inline aggregation was pulled out into a pure rollupRecap(rows), and both the authed recap card and the public TV read it. A second rollup would have been easy and would have drifted, and the failure mode is uniquely bad here because both surfaces are visible AT THE SAME TIME in the same room — someone holding the recap card on their phone while the TV shows the standings, disagreeing about who is winning. The order of the players array became load-bearing in the process (the TV reads index 0 as the leader), so the MVP rule is now covered by tests rather than being an implementation detail of one card. One piece of existing behaviour the tests pin rather than change: once ANY game exists the mvp is whoever tops the ranking, so a crown can sit on a 0W row on a night where nothing has been won yet.
- Render the pack's TV view, never navigate to it (2026-07-28): the original spec said the event TV route should redirect (replace nav) to the pack's own TV url. Rendering the child in place was chosen instead, and the difference matters on a device nobody is holding. A redirect means the address bar no longer says /e/:id/tv, so the TV is now pinned to ONE pack again and has quietly lost the thing it was set up to do; recovering needs a human. Even with replace-nav, the route that redirects and the route redirected to keep swapping the history entry, and the Back button on a TV remote is not something anyone should have to think about. Rendering keeps the night's address on screen for the whole night, which is the entire feature. The consequence to hold onto: the child MOUNTS AND UNMOUNTS on every switch, so a child that leaks a timer leaks it once per pack change across a whole evening. Beerio is the one that polls on its own timer (correctly, as a vendored pack that owns its loop), so its cleanup was verified in a browser rather than by reading the code: its request count is frozen for 7 seconds after the swap, with a 3-second poll, checked only after first proving the poll had been running.
- Beerio needed a completion marker before it could be ranked at all (2026-07-28): the other four packs carry an explicit status that reaches "completed" and drops them out of the running. Beerio had an open signal (the room code on the event) and a completion signal (the /beerio-complete route that writes the standings snapshot) with nothing joining them, so a room stayed "open" forever once opened and would have won the TV for the rest of the crew's history. Three options were on the table: a recency window ("a room older than N hours is dead"), reading the vendored state blob to work out whether the tournament finished, or a stamp. The window is a guess that is wrong at both ends — it kills a long night and resurrects a dead room the next morning — and reading the blob was already rejected once, on 07-27, for the guest-linking work: it is the vendored engine's opaque shape, keyed by a REUSABLE live-session code with no link to a finished match. So: events.beerioCompletedAt, set at completion, cleared when a new room opens, and the room counts as live when the code is set and (there is no stamp OR beerio_sessions.updatedAt is newer than it). The last clause is what makes a second tournament on the same code work with no extra mechanism: the engine writes state, updatedAt passes the stamp, the room is live again. One deliberate extra rule: a code with NO beerio_sessions row never wins, because there is no board to draw and the TV would show a spinner for a room that does not exist. All of this lives in beerio-gn.ts, which is ours; BeerioApp.tsx and beerio.ts stay byte-stable per the 1:1 port rule.
- The tiebreak is declared in code, not left to Postgres (2026-07-28): two candidates genuinely can carry the same millisecond, and without a fixed order the winner depends on row order, which is not stable across queries. That is not a cosmetic problem here. The TV refetches on every relevant broadcast, so an unstable comparator makes the screen flip between two packs on consecutive refetches, and a TV that changes its mind every few seconds reads as broken rather than as a tie. The order is bracket, beerio, smash, mariokart, marioparty, pingpong, asserted in tests both ways round (including with the candidate array reversed, so the test cannot pass by accident of insertion order). Same reasoning for a missing timestamp: it sorts oldest rather than being treated as "unknown, might be newest", so a row with no updatedAt can never win by accident.
- What the bracket updatedAt default does to existing rows, stated here rather than left to be rediscovered (2026-07-28): `ALTER TABLE brackets ADD COLUMN updated_at timestamp NOT NULL DEFAULT now()` stamps EVERY historical bracket with the migration time, so for one moment every bracket the crew has ever played looks freshly touched. It does not matter, and the reason is worth writing down so nobody "fixes" it with a backfill: completed brackets are filtered out of the resolver BEFORE anything is ranked, so a finished tournament's timestamp is never compared against anything, and any bracket that is still live genuinely is the current one. The only observable effect would be on an event with two live things where one is an old un-finished bracket, which is a state that also resolves itself the moment anyone scores anything. Full deploy SQL, if drizzle-kit's push no-ops in CI (see the standing BUGS watch): `ALTER TABLE brackets ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS updated_at timestamp NOT NULL DEFAULT now();` and `ALTER TABLE events ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS beerio_completed_at timestamp;`
- One broadcast fixed two screens that were both quietly wrong (2026-07-28): `event_session_changed` existed in the shared WsEvent union, EventPage listened for it, and exactly ONE place in the entire server ever sent it (the Beerio room-open route). So a host starting Smash from their phone updated nothing on anyone else's event page: smash_updated fired, and EventPage does not listen for that. Nobody had reported it, because the person starting the session is looking at the session, not at the event page. Building the TV resolver forced the gap into the open, since the resolver needs exactly the same signal for exactly the same reason, and emitting it from pack-runtime's startSession and from saveState's transition to "completed" covers all four packs in one place. Brackets emit it on create, score AND undo, which is broader than the plan's "create and complete": a recorded result moves the bracket to the front of "most recently touched", and bracket_updated is keyed by bracketId while both the event page and the event TV watch by eventId, so without it the new updatedAt ranking would only ever be observed by accident. The undo case is the interesting one — it can bring a COMPLETED bracket back to life, taking it from filtered-out to the freshest thing on the night.
- The live pack session is the one screen that must NOT be cached (2026-07-28, cleanup phase 6): the backlog entry for this phase said to build usePackSession on top of the 8a cache helper so the pack pages would inherit the same instant-return behaviour as everything else. That was not done, and the reason is worth keeping. Everywhere else in the app a stale render is cosmetic and corrects itself a moment later. A live pack screen is different in kind, because it is not just showing state, it is offering BUTTONS THAT WRITE: painting a cached roster means a host can tap a player who is no longer in the session, or record a result into a session that has already been replaced, and the write lands before the revalidation arrives to say otherwise. The launch context is excluded for the same reason, since it carries the member list the setup roster is built from and the `live` flag that decides whether the page offers to start at all. So the pack pages fetch fresh every time and are now the only screens that still show a loading state on a return visit. The cost is small (you reach these screens once a night and then stay on them) and the alternative is a wrong write, which is the one failure this app cannot take back cleanly.
- Unifying four copies is how the Mario Party gap was found (2026-07-28, cleanup phase 6): Mario Party was the only pack that started its session through the generic call() helper rather than a startSession() of its own, so it silently lacked the 409 confirm-and-replace dance, and the server lacked the matching `!req.body?.force` check that the other three had always had. The combination meant an in-progress Mario Party session could never be replaced: the host got the raw 409 text on the setup screen and the only way out was to finish or abandon the night. Nobody had reported it, which is the point. A fourth hand-copied implementation does not announce that it is missing a branch the other three have; it just quietly does less, and it takes putting the four side by side to see the hole. That is the argument for this whole phase, and it is worth remembering the next time four similar screens look like they are "basically the same already".
- A cached identity is a rendering hint, never a permission (2026-07-28, cleanup phase 8a): the app no longer waits for /api/auth/me before it will draw anything, which means for a fraction of a second it can be drawing a screen based on who you were last time rather than who the server says you are. That is safe, and the reason needs to survive in writing so a later session does not "harden" it back into a blocking gate: the cached Me decides what the UI DRAWS, never what the user may DO. Every endpoint authenticates the session cookie itself, server-side, on every request, so a stale cached identity can produce a briefly wrong-looking screen and nothing else. Trading a guaranteed full-screen wait on every launch for a rare half-second of a stale display name is the right way round. The nuance that took a second pass: `undefined` and `null` must stay DIFFERENT states. Collapsing them makes a cold cache render as signed-out, which would flash the login screen at every signed-in user, and since a deploy sweeps the cache by design that would be everyone after every deploy. Flashing a login screen at someone who is signed in is worse than the spinner this phase existed to remove, so an unknown answer still waits and only a KNOWN one paints early.
- Cached content beats a failed refresh (2026-07-28, cleanup phase 8a): wiring the cache in exposed the same latent bug on several pages, which had all been written as `if (error) return <Error/>` BEFORE `if (!data) return <Loading/>`. That ordering is harmless when a page has nothing to show until it fetches, and actively wrong once it does: a revalidation failing (phone lost signal, Render still waking) would throw away a crew page or an event page that was already on screen and perfectly readable, and replace it with an error. The rule now is that data wins: the error screen only appears when there is genuinely nothing to render, and a failed background refresh just means what you are looking at is a few seconds old. This is the whole reason to have a cache on a flaky phone connection, so getting the branch order wrong would have cancelled out most of the benefit.
- The browser checks were built because the acceptance criteria were browser-only (2026-07-28, cleanup phase 8a): the phase is defined in terms nobody can verify by reading code, "the warm launch should paint the real UI with no Loading anywhere", so it was checked in a real browser instead of asserted. Chromium was driven over CDP against the REAL built bundle plus a stub API with a deliberate 250ms delay, because without the delay a fetch can resolve before first paint and hide the exact behaviour under test. Worth recording that the first run produced a FALSE PASS: the logged-out check matched "Your name" on the still-logged-in home page and reported green. The actual fault was in the harness, not the app (a `pkill -f stub-server.mjs` matched the shell running the edit and killed it, so the stub never gained its logout switch and was quietly answering 200), but a check that passes for the wrong reason is worse than one that fails, and it was only caught by reading the matched text rather than the verdict. Fixed, the same check correctly finds the login screen and an empty cache. The scripts were not committed, for the same reasons phase 4 gave: they depend on a browser path that is an environment detail, and a test needing a running preview server does not belong in the tsx unit-test glob.
- Cold-start latency: widen the keep-warm window, do not build a service worker (2026-07-28, cleanup phase 8b, decided by James). Of the three options put to him, he picked the cheapest: the ping window goes from evenings only to noon-2am Central, ~310 instance-hours a month to ~487 against the free 750. What that buys is that the app is already awake for the hours anyone actually opens it, so the 30-60 second cold start stops being the normal experience and becomes a 3am edge case. What it does NOT buy is a fix: outside the window the first request for index.html still waits for Render to spin up, and no client-side work can change that, because nothing renders before the HTML arrives. THE SERVICE WORKER WAS NOT CHOSEN and must not be slipped into a later phase as an optimisation. It is the only option that genuinely fixes a cold first load, since the shell would paint from cache while the data waits for the server, but it carries the classic footgun: a service worker that caches too eagerly pins users to an old app version, and being stuck on stale code on a game night is worse than waiting thirty seconds for a fresh one. If it is ever revisited it needs its own session with an explicit update strategy, and it would be reversing the 2026-07-18 decision on NEW grounds rather than changing our minds: that call rested on the app being online-only, which is still true of the data and no longer true of the shell. Paying for a non-sleeping tier stays available and stays James's alone. The event-aware warmer under IDEAS still composes with this and would trade some of the 487 hours back.
- What the pack runtime does NOT absorb (2026-07-28, cleanup phase 3): the tempting version of this refactor puts the whole pack behind one interface and leaves each file a config block. That was rejected. Three things stay per-pack on purpose. The LEDGER UNIT, because it is a real modelling difference and not duplication: a Smash FFA game and a Mario Kart race are game-as-unit, a best-of series and a ping pong match are match-as-unit, a Mario Party board is board-as-unit, and flattening them would either lose per-unit detail or invent it. The ROUTES and their request validation, because each pack's body shapes and 400 messages are part of its behaviour and a shared validator would drift into a lowest common denominator. And the SESSION PAYLOAD EXTRAS, since the runtime supplies the envelope (status, groupId, the state spread) and the pack supplies whatever its own screen reads. What did move is only the plumbing that was character-for-character identical across four files. The one place a judgement call went the other way is materializeUnit: the find-or-insert-the-matches-row dance was repeated six times (Smash game and series, MK game and series, MP board, PP match) and is genuinely identical, so it moved, leaving each materialize* to decide the lines and the label, which is the part that differs. Two constraints were held explicitly rather than trusted: Smash stays on smash_sessions keyed by eventId alone while the other three share game_sessions keyed (eventId, pack), because collapsing them is a data migration wearing a refactor costume; and ledgerKey output is unchanged character for character, because a changed key does not error, it just orphans the existing rows and the leaderboard quietly stops matching history. Both are asserted in tests, the second against strings captured from the old code before it was touched. Separately, wsType stopped being a `string` and became a four-member literal union: a typo'd one used to be undetectable until someone noticed a TV screen had stopped updating, and it is now a compile error.
- Tests were written against the code being replaced, not the replacement (2026-07-28, cleanup phase 3): the phase's own instruction was to confirm the tests pass against the CURRENT code first, since a test written afterwards only canonises whatever the change did. Following that literally needed a mechanism, because ledgerKey was a private function in each of the four pack files and there was nothing importable to assert against. The four were temporarily exported, a throwaway test asserted the exact strings they produced, that ran green, and only then did the refactor start; the throwaway was deleted and the committed test asserts the SAME strings against the runtime. This mattered more than it sounds: it is the difference between proving the key shape is unchanged and merely proving the new code is self-consistent. The same reasoning drove the verification at the end. Rather than reading the four files and declaring the routes unchanged, the actual Express router stacks were enumerated on this tree and on HEAD (in a throwaway git worktree) and diffed: 52 routes, with method, path and handler count, identical. Reading finds what you expect; the diff finds what you did not.
- Cross-crew show-up record excludes quick-play crews (2026-07-28, cleanup phase 7): the flake stat asks whether you turned up when you said you would, which needs someone to have let down. A personal quick-play crew is a hidden crew of one, created so scoring and TV work without a real crew; there is no RSVP to break and no night that can be missed, so including them would pad the denominator with unmissable nights and quietly inflate everyone's show rate. Lifetime totals still count quick play, because those games were genuinely played. The related call: /me/stats was NOT repointed at aggFor as the cleanup plan proposed. The stated problem behind that instruction was that /me/stats ran its own aggregation and so could disagree with the profile, but that had already been fixed when the shared Agg gained the deep stats, and repointing would have introduced two new faults: a second scan of the same rows to rebuild byFormat and byCrew, which aggFor does not return, and a silent data loss, because aggFor scopes by current memberships while the endpoint scopes by userId, and leaving a crew deletes the membership while leaving the participant rows behind.
